People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Or someone says something and I’ll repeat a phrase back instead of answering normally. It’s more like I suddenly feel I need to put them in that exact order or pattern, even if I’m supposed to be teaching or listening in a meeting. Then I notice I’ve stopped what I was doing, and I feel embarrassed because it interrupts things.\n\nDoctor: When you repeat a phrase back instead of answering normally, does the phrase you repeat seem related to what was just said to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, usually it’s something they’ve just said. Yes, I think it does sometimes. If I stop in the middle of a lesson or a meeting to line up markers or papers just so, people lose the thread of what I was saying. It’s more that she’ll say my name, or make a comment about what I’m doing. I don’t think I usually have trouble explaining myself. Sometimes I might lose my train of thought if I’m stressed, but I haven’t really noticed my thoughts becoming hard to follow or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities that seem unusual or out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. Then I realize it’s interrupted the conversation and I feel embarrassed, because it probably doesn’t make much sense to them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the way you repeat phrases back or arrange things in a particular pattern affects how others understand what you're trying to communicate?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does sometimes. If I stop in the middle of a lesson or a meeting to line up markers or papers just so, people lose the thread of what I was saying. And when I repeat someone’s words back instead of answering, it can come across like I’m not following them. I usually realize it after a moment and feel embarrassed, because it interrupts what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when your thoughts are not logically connected?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t usually notice my thoughts being illogical at the time. Sometimes other people look confused, so maybe I’m not explaining myself well, but I wouldn’t say I can clearly tell when my thoughts aren’t connected.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It still feels like they’re meant for me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a conversation where you felt like your responses weren't making sense to the other person?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. Not exactly that I’m trying to be confusing, but in lessons or meetings I’ll suddenly stop what I’m doing and start arranging markers or papers in a very particular way. Or someone will say something and I’ll sort of repeat their phrase back instead of actually answering. Then I realize it’s interrupted the conversation and I feel embarrassed, because it probably doesn’t make much sense to them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the way you repeat phrases back or arrange things in a particular pattern affects how others understand what you're trying to communicate?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does sometimes. If I stop in the middle of a lesson or a meeting to line up markers or papers just so, people lose the thread of what I was saying. And when I repeat someone’s words back instead of answering, it can come across like I’m not following them. I usually realize it after a moment and feel embarrassed, because it interrupts what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when your thoughts are not logically connected?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t usually notice my thoughts being illogical at the time. Sometimes other people look confused, so maybe I’m not explaining myself well, but I wouldn’t say I can clearly tell when my thoughts aren’t connected.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it difficult to stay on topic when speaking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes, at times. It’s not constant, but in lessons or art-fair meetings I can lose the thread because I stop mid-task to rearrange markers or papers in a very particular way. Or someone says something and I repeat part of their phrase instead of answering properly. Then I realize I’ve gone off from what I was meant to be saying, and I feel embarrassed because it interrupts things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having this feeling I can’t really shake at work. I’m a teacher, and sometimes staff emails or the morning announcements feel like they’re… coded, somehow, like they’re really about me. My colleagues have pointed out they’re sent to everyone, and I understand that, but the feeling still sticks with me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this feeling that messages are coded about you?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. The coded-message worries started around then… and the voice I sometimes answer has been happening in that same time too.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a voice you sometimes answer, can you hear this voice?\n\nPatient: Yes. Not all the time, but sometimes when it’s quiet at home, or after my classroom has emptied, I hear a woman’s voice. She’ll say my name, or make a short comment about what I’m doing. It’s clear enough that I sometimes answer her before I realize there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Does the woman's voice ever tell you to do something?\n\nPatient: No, not really telling me to do things. It’s more that she’ll say my name, or make a comment about what I’m doing. It’s clear enough that I sometimes answer, and then I realize there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to follow your own thoughts or express them to others in a way that feels coherent?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I usually have trouble explaining myself. Sometimes I might lose my train of thought if I’m stressed, but I haven’t really noticed my thoughts becoming hard to follow or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities that seem unusual or out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. During lessons or meetings for the art fair, I’ll sometimes stop what I’m doing and start rearranging markers or papers into a very particular pattern. Or someone says something and I’ll repeat a phrase back instead of answering normally. It interrupts things, and then I feel embarrassed because it’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: When you're rearranging markers or papers into a particular pattern, are you aware of why you're doing it or is it just something you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not really aware of a clear reason. It’s more like I suddenly feel I need to put them in that exact order or pattern, even if I’m supposed to be teaching or listening in a meeting. Then I notice I’ve stopped what I was doing, and I feel embarrassed because it interrupts things.\n\nDoctor: When you repeat a phrase back instead of answering normally, does the phrase you repeat seem related to what was just said to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, usually it’s something they’ve just said. I’ll sort of echo a phrase back instead of giving an actual answer. It’s not meant to be rude—I just catch myself doing it, and then I feel embarrassed because it interrupts the lesson or the meeting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed my thoughts feeling disconnected exactly. Sometimes I lose the thread of what I was doing, but I wouldn’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you had trouble getting your thoughts into words, or when what you said seemed jumbled or hard for others to understand?\n\nPatient: I can’t think of a clear example. I mean, I sometimes pause or lose what I was about to say, but I haven’t really noticed my words coming out jumbled, or people saying they couldn’t understand me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voice you hear or the feelings of coded messages are definitely real, even when others tell you they're not?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually why I came in. I keep feeling like certain staff emails or the morning announcements at school have coded messages about me. My colleagues have shown me they were sent to everyone, and I can see that, but the feeling doesn’t really go away. It still feels like they’re meant for me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a conversation where you felt like your responses weren't making sense to the other person?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. If I stop in the middle of a lesson or a meeting to line up markers or papers just so, people lose the thread of what I was saying. And when I repeat someone’s words back instead of answering, it can come across like I’m not following them. I usually realize it after a moment and feel embarrassed, because it interrupts what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when your thoughts are not logically connected?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t usually notice my thoughts being illogical at the time. Sometimes other people look confused, so maybe I’m not explaining myself well, but I wouldn’t say I can clearly tell when my thoughts aren’t connected.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it difficult to stay on topic when speaking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes, at times. It’s not constant, but in lessons or art-fair meetings I can lose the thread because I stop mid-task to rearrange markers or papers in a very particular way. Or someone says something and I repeat part of their phrase instead of answering properly. Then I realize I’ve gone off from what I was meant to be saying, and I feel embarrassed because it interrupts things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are usually organized and make sense to you?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yes, my thoughts make sense to me. I don’t usually feel like they’re jumbled while I’m thinking them. It’s not constant, but in lessons or art-fair meetings I can lose the thread because I stop mid-task to rearrange markers or papers in a very particular way. I don’t usually feel like they’re jumbled while I’m thinking them. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having this feeling I can’t really shake at work. I’m a teacher, and sometimes staff emails or the morning announcements feel like they’re… coded, somehow, like they’re really about me. My colleagues have pointed out they’re sent to everyone, and I understand that, but the feeling still sticks with me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this feeling that messages are coded about you?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. The coded-message worries started around then… and the voice I sometimes answer has been happening in that same time too.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a voice you sometimes answer, can you hear this voice?\n\nPatient: Yes. Not all the time, but sometimes when it’s quiet at home, or after my classroom has emptied, I hear a woman’s voice. She’ll say my name, or make a short comment about what I’m doing. It’s clear enough that I sometimes answer her before I realize there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Does the woman's voice ever tell you to do something?\n\nPatient: No, not really telling me to do things. It’s more that she’ll say my name, or make a comment about what I’m doing. It’s clear enough that I sometimes answer, and then I realize there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to follow your own thoughts or express them to others in a way that feels coherent?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I usually have trouble explaining myself. Sometimes I might lose my train of thought if I’m stressed, but I haven’t really noticed my thoughts becoming hard to follow or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities that seem unusual or out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. During lessons or meetings for the art fair, I’ll sometimes stop what I’m doing and start rearranging markers or papers into a very particular pattern. Or someone says something and I’ll repeat a phrase back instead of answering normally. It interrupts things, and then I feel embarrassed because it’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: When you're rearranging markers or papers into a particular pattern, are you aware of why you're doing it or is it just something you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not really aware of a clear reason. It’s more like I suddenly feel I need to put them in that exact order or pattern, even if I’m supposed to be teaching or listening in a meeting. Then I notice I’ve stopped what I was doing, and I feel embarrassed because it interrupts things.\n\nDoctor: When you repeat a phrase back instead of answering normally, does the phrase you repeat seem related to what was just said to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, usually it’s something they’ve just said. I’ll sort of echo a phrase back instead of giving an actual answer. It’s not meant to be rude—I just catch myself doing it, and then I feel embarrassed because it interrupts the lesson or the meeting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed my thoughts feeling disconnected exactly. Sometimes I lose the thread of what I was doing, but I wouldn’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you had trouble getting your thoughts into words, or when what you said seemed jumbled or hard for others to understand?\n\nPatient: I can’t think of a clear example. I mean, I sometimes pause or lose what I was about to say, but I haven’t really noticed my words coming out jumbled, or people saying they couldn’t understand me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voice you hear or the feelings of coded messages are definitely real, even when others tell you they're not?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually why I came in. I keep feeling like certain staff emails or the morning announcements at school have coded messages about me. My colleagues have shown me they were sent to everyone, and I can see that, but the feeling doesn’t really go away. It still feels like they’re meant for me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a conversation where you felt like your responses weren't making sense to the other person?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. Not exactly that I’m trying to be confusing, but in lessons or meetings I’ll suddenly stop what I’m doing and start arranging markers or papers in a very particular way. Or someone will say something and I’ll sort of repeat their phrase back instead of actually answering. Then I realize it’s interrupted the conversation and I feel embarrassed, because it probably doesn’t make much sense to them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the way you repeat phrases back or arrange things in a particular pattern affects how others understand what you're trying to communicate?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does sometimes. If I stop in the middle of a lesson or a meeting to line up markers or papers just so, people lose the thread of what I was saying. And when I repeat someone’s words back instead of answering, it can come across like I’m not following them. I usually realize it after a moment and feel embarrassed, because it interrupts what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when your thoughts are not logically connected?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t usually notice my thoughts being illogical at the time. Sometimes other people look confused, so maybe I’m not explaining myself well, but I wouldn’t say I can clearly tell when my thoughts aren’t connected.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it difficult to stay on topic when speaking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes, at times. It’s not constant, but in lessons or art-fair meetings I can lose the thread because I stop mid-task to rearrange markers or papers in a very particular way. Then I realize I’ve gone off from what I was meant to be saying, and I feel embarrassed because it interrupts things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are usually organized and make sense to you?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yes, my thoughts make sense to me. I don’t usually feel like they’re jumbled while I’m thinking them. It’s more that sometimes I realize afterward that I didn’t explain myself clearly, especially if someone looks confused.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when your thoughts are not making sense to you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. The coded-message worries started around then… and the voice I sometimes answer has been happening in that same time too.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a voice you sometimes answer, can you hear this voice?\n\nPatient: Yes. Not all the time, but sometimes when it’s quiet at home, or after my classroom has emptied, I hear a woman’s voice. She’ll say my name, or make a short comment about what I’m doing. It’s clear enough that I sometimes answer her before I realize there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Does the woman's voice ever tell you to do something?\n\nPatient: No, not really telling me to do things. It’s more that she’ll say my name, or make a comment about what I’m doing. It’s clear enough that I sometimes answer, and then I realize there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to follow your own thoughts or express them to others in a way that feels coherent?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I usually have trouble explaining myself. Sometimes I might lose my train of thought if I’m stressed, but I haven’t really noticed my thoughts becoming hard to follow or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities that seem unusual or out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. During lessons or meetings for the art fair, I’ll sometimes stop what I’m doing and start rearranging markers or papers into a very particular pattern. It interrupts things, and then I feel embarrassed because it’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: When you're rearranging markers or papers into a particular pattern, are you aware of why you're doing it or is it just something you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not really aware of a clear reason. It’s more like I suddenly feel I need to put them in that exact order or pattern, even if I’m supposed to be teaching or listening in a meeting. Then I notice I’ve stopped what I was doing, and I feel embarrassed because it interrupts things.\n\nDoctor: When you repeat a phrase back instead of answering normally, does the phrase you repeat seem related to what was just said to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, usually it’s something they’ve just said. I’ll sort of echo a phrase back instead of giving an actual answer. It’s not meant to be rude—I just catch myself doing it, and then I feel embarrassed because it interrupts the lesson or the meeting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed my thoughts feeling disconnected exactly. Sometimes I lose the thread of what I was doing, but I wouldn’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you had trouble getting your thoughts into words, or when what you said seemed jumbled or hard for others to understand?\n\nPatient: I can’t think of a clear example. I mean, I sometimes pause or lose what I was about to say, but I haven’t really noticed my words coming out jumbled, or people saying they couldn’t understand me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voice you hear or the feelings of coded messages are definitely real, even when others tell you they're not?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually why I came in. It’s not constant, but in lessons or art-fair meetings I can lose the thread because I stop mid-task to rearrange markers or papers in a very particular way. Then I realize I’ve gone off from what I was meant to be saying, and I feel embarrassed because it interrupts things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are usually organized and make sense to you?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yes, my thoughts make sense to me. I don’t usually feel like they’re jumbled while I’m thinking them. I keep feeling like certain staff emails or the morning announcements at school have coded messages about me. My colleagues have shown me they were sent to everyone, and I can see that, but the feeling doesn’t really go away. It still feels like they’re meant for me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a conversation where you felt like your responses weren't making sense to the other person?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. Not exactly that I’m trying to be confusing, but in lessons or meetings I’ll suddenly stop what I’m doing and start arranging markers or papers in a very particular way. Or someone will say something and I’ll sort of repeat their phrase back instead of actually answering. Then I realize it’s interrupted the conversation and I feel embarrassed, because it probably doesn’t make much sense to them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the way you repeat phrases back or arrange things in a particular pattern affects how others understand what you're trying to communicate?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does sometimes. If I stop in the middle of a lesson or a meeting to line up markers or papers just so, people lose the thread of what I was saying. And when I repeat someone’s words back instead of answering, it can come across like I’m not following them. I usually realize it after a moment and feel embarrassed, because it interrupts what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when your thoughts are not logically connected?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t usually notice my thoughts being illogical at the time. Sometimes other people look confused, so maybe I’m not explaining myself well, but I wouldn’t say I can clearly tell when my thoughts aren’t connected.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it difficult to stay on topic when speaking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes, at times. It’s not constant, but in lessons or art-fair meetings I can lose the thread because I stop mid-task to rearrange markers or papers in a very particular way. Or someone says something and I repeat part of their phrase instead of answering properly. Then I realize I’ve gone off from what I was meant to be saying, and I feel embarrassed because it interrupts things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are usually organized and make sense to you?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yes, my thoughts make sense to me. I don’t usually feel like they’re jumbled while I’m thinking them. It’s more that sometimes I realize afterward that I didn’t explain myself clearly, especially if someone looks confused.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when your thoughts are not making sense to you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, my thoughts usually make sense to me while I’m having them. It’s more afterward that I might wonder if I explained it badly, but I don’t often feel confused by my own thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when your thoughts are fragmented or difficult for you to follow?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no. My thoughts can wander or I might lose track of what I was saying, but I wouldn’t say they feel fragmented or hard for me to follow inside my own head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are ever confusing to yourself, even if just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Maybe briefly, like if I’m tired or distracted, I might have to stop and think, “What was I trying to say?” But I don’t usually feel confused by my own thoughts themselves. It’s more losing my train of thought for a moment.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
